===Original===
To

Vincent Starrett

===Chinese===
致

文森特·斯塔雷特（Vincent Starrett）

## Note / 作者注

===Original===
One of the schoolmasters in *The Secret Glory* has views on the subject of football similar to those entertained by a well-known schoolmaster whose Biography appeared many years ago. That is the only link between the villain of invention and the good man of real life.

===Chinese===
《隐秘的荣耀》（*The Secret Glory*）中有一位教师，其对足球问题的见解，与多年前有传记问世的一位著名教师所持的见解相似。这便是虚构中的恶人与现实中的善人之间唯一的关联。

## Preface / 序言

===Original===
Some years ago I met my old master, Sir Frank Benson—he was Mr. F. R. Benson then—and he asked me in his friendly way what I had been doing lately.

“I am just finishing a book,” I replied, “a book that everybody will hate.”

“As usual,” said the Don Quixote of our English stage—if I knew any nobler title to bestow upon him, I would bestow it—“as usual; running your head against a stone wall!”

===Chinese===
几年前，我遇见了我昔日的班主弗兰克·本森爵士（Sir Frank Benson）——那时他还是F. R. 本森先生——他照例亲切地问起我近来在做些什么。

“我刚写完一本书，”我答道，“一本人人都会讨厌的书。”

“照例如此，”我们英国戏剧界的堂吉诃德（Don Quixote）说道——倘若我知道有什么更尊贵的称号可以奉赠给他，我必定奉赠——“照例如此；又拿脑袋往石墙上撞！”

===Original===
Well, I don’t know about “as usual”; there may be something to be said for the personal criticism or there may not; but it has struck me that Sir Frank’s remark is a very good description of *The Secret Glory*, the book I had in mind as I talked to him. It is emphatically the history of an unfortunate fellow who ran his head against stone walls from the beginning to the end. He could think nothing and do nothing after the common fashion of the world; even when he “went wrong,” he did so in a highly unusual and eccentric manner. It will be for the reader to determine whether he were a saint who had lost his way in the centuries or merely an undeveloped lunatic; I hold no passionate view on either side. In every age, there are people great and small for whom the times are out of joint, for whom everything is, somehow, wrong and askew. Consider Hamlet; an amiable man and an intelligent man. But what a mess he made of it! Fortunately, my hero—or idiot, which you will—was not called upon to intermeddle with affairs of State, and so only brought himself to grief: if it were grief; for the least chink of the door should be kept open, I am inclined to hold, for the other point of view. I have just been rereading Kipling’s *The Miracle of Purun Bhagat*, the tale of the Brahmin Prime Minister of the Native State in India, who saw all the world and the glory of it, in the West as well as in the East, and suddenly abjured all to become a hermit in the wood. Was he mad, or was he supremely wise? It is just a matter of opinion.

===Chinese===
唉，“照例如此”究竟是否属实，我不敢断言；这句针对我个人的批评或许有几分道理，或许毫无道理；但我总觉得，本森爵士这句话，恰好可以拿来形容《隐秘的荣耀》（*The Secret Glory*）——我同他交谈时心里想着的正是这本书。它不折不扣是一个不幸家伙的历史：此人从开头到结尾都在拿脑袋撞石墙。无论思想还是行事，他都无法循世人的寻常路数；就连“走上邪路”的时候，他走的也是一副极不寻常、乖僻古怪的路子。他究竟是迷失于千百个世纪之间的圣徒，抑或不过是一个心智未开的疯子，且由读者自行裁断；我对哪一方都不抱激烈的成见。无论哪个时代，总有人——大的小的——生逢时世脱节之秋，在他们眼中，万事万物不知怎么都错了位、歪了斜。想想哈姆雷特（Hamlet）吧；一个和蔼可亲的人，一个聪明人。可他把事情搞成了什么一团糟！所幸，我的主人公——或者说白痴，随你怎么叫——并未被召去插手国事，所以只是害了他自己：如果那也算害的话；因为我倾向于认为，应当为另一种见解留出哪怕最窄的一道门缝。我近来重读吉卜林（Kipling）先生的《普伦·巴加特的奇迹》（*The Miracle of Purun Bhagat*），讲的是印度一个土邦的婆罗门宰相：此公阅尽尘世与尘世的一切荣光——西方看过，东方也看过——忽而弃绝一切，遁入林中做了隐士。他是疯了，还是智慧到了极处？这只是个见仁见智的问题。

===Original===
The origin and genesis of *The Secret Glory* were odd enough. Once on a time, I read the life of a famous schoolmaster, one of the most notable schoolmasters of these later days. I believe he was an excellent man in every way; but, somehow, that “Life” got on my nerves. I thought that the School Songs—for which, amongst other things, this master was famous—were drivel; I thought his views about football, regarded, not as a good game, but as the discipline and guide of life, were rot, and poisonous rot at that. In a word, the “Life” of this excellent man got my back up.

Very good. The year after, schoolmasters and football had ceased to engage my attention. I was deeply interested in a curious and minute investigation of the wonderful legend of the Holy Grail; or rather, in one aspect of that extraordinary complex. My researches led me to the connection of the Grail Legend with the vanished Celtic Church which held the field in Britain in the fifth and sixth and seventh centuries; I undertook an extraordinary and fascinating journey into a misty and uncertain region of Christian history. I must not say more here, lest—as Nurse says to the troublesome and persistent child—I “begin all over again”; but, indeed, it was a voyage on perilous seas, a journey to faery lands forlorn—and I would declare, by the way, my conviction that if there had been no Celtic Church, Keats could never have written those lines of tremendous evocation and incantation.

===Chinese===
《隐秘的荣耀》的来历与缘起，也足够古怪。从前有一回，我读到一位著名教师的传记——他是晚近最负盛名的教师之一。我相信此公各方面都堪称卓越；可不知怎的，那部《传记》却搅得我心神不宁。我觉得那些校园歌曲（School Songs）——除了别的而外，这位先生正以此闻名——不过是些梦呓般的蠢话；我觉得他关于足球的种种高见——不把足球看作一项好游戏，而是把它当作人生的训诫与指南——纯属胡言，而且是有毒的胡言。总而言之，这位卓越人士的《传记》叫我火冒三丈。

很好。第二年，教师与足球已不再占据我的心思。我深深地沉浸于对圣杯（the Holy Grail）这一奇妙传说的一项别致而精微的考察之中；说得确切些，是沉浸于那个非同寻常的复合体的一面。我的研究把我引向圣杯传说与已然消逝的凯尔特教会（Celtic Church）之间的关联——那个教会在五、六、七三个世纪里称雄不列颠；于是我踏上一段非同凡响、令人心醉的旅程，走进基督教历史中一片雾气弥漫、渺茫难定的地域。此处不便多言，惟恐——正如保姆对那个纠缠不休的顽童所说——我又“从头再讲一遍”；然而说真的，那确乎是一次在凶险海上的航行，一趟去往孤绝仙境的旅程——顺带说一句，我愿申明我的信念：倘若没有凯尔特教会，济慈（Keats）断然写不出那些具有巨大感召力与咒语般力量的诗句。

===Original===
Again; very good. The year after, it came upon me to write a book. And I hit upon an original plan; or so I thought. I took my dislike of the good schoolmaster’s “Life,” I took my knowledge of Celtic mysteries—and combined my information.

Original, this plan! It was all thought of years before I was born. Do you remember the critic of the *Eatanswill Gazette*? He had to review for that admirable journal a work on Chinese Metaphysics. Mr. Pott tells the story of the article.

“He read up for the subject, at my desire, in the *Encyclopædia Britannica*… he read for metaphysics under the letter *M*, and for China under the letter *C*, and combined his information!”

===Chinese===
又一次；很好。再过一年，我动了写一本书的念头，而且想出一个别出心裁的计划；至少我自己是这么想的。我拾起对那位善良教师的《传记》的厌恶，拾起我对凯尔特秘义的知识——然后把材料合并起来。

独创，这个计划！其实早在若干年前、我尚未出生之时，就有人把它想出来了。你还记得《伊坦斯维尔公报》（*Eatanswill Gazette*）的那位评论家吗？他得为那份令人钦佩的报纸撰写一篇关于中国形而上学的书评。波特先生（Mr. Pott）讲述了那篇文章的来历。

“他应我的要求，为这个题目研读了《大英百科全书》（*Encyclopædia Britannica*）……他在字母M之下查‘形而上学’，在字母C之下查‘中国’，然后把材料合并起来！”

## I / I

### I / I

===Original===
A heavy cloud passed swiftly away before the wind that came with the night, and far in a clear sky the evening star shone with pure brightness, a gleaming world set high above the dark earth and the black shadows in the lane. In the ending of October a great storm had blown from the west, and it was through the bare boughs of a twisted oak that Ambrose Meyrick saw the silver light of the star. As the last faint flash died in the sky he leaned against a gate and gazed upward; and then his eyes fell on the dull and weary undulations of the land, the vast circle of dun ploughland and grey meadow bounded by a dim horizon, dreary as a prison wall. He remembered with a start how late it must be; he should have been back an hour before, and he was still in the open country, a mile away at least from the outskirts of Lupton. He turned from the star and began to walk as quickly as he could along the lane through the puddles and the sticky clay, soaked with three weeks’ heavy rain.

===Chinese===
一朵浓云被入夜的长风急急赶散，晴空深处，长庚星闪烁着纯净的光辉，俨然一个发光的小世界，高悬在黑沉沉的大地与小路的暗影之上。十月将尽，一场大风暴自西面吹来；安布罗斯·梅里克（Ambrose Meyrick）正是从一株虬曲老橡树光秃秃的枝柯间，望见那星子的银光。当天边最后一抹微弱的闪光熄灭时，他倚着一扇栅门向天凝望；随后目光落在这片单调而疲惫的丘陵地上——辽阔的一环黄褐色耕地与灰白草场，被一道模糊的地平线圈住，阴郁得像一堵监狱的高墙。他猛然惊觉此刻该有多晚了；他本该在一个钟头以前回去，而人还在旷野里，离卢普顿（Lupton）城郊至少有一英里。他从星辰旁收回目光，沿着小路在积水和黏泥之间尽量加快脚步——黏泥已被连下三个礼拜的大雨泡得稀烂。

===Original===
He saw at last the faint lamps of the nearest streets where the shoemakers lived and he tramped hurriedly through this wretched quarter, past its penny shops, its raw public-house, its rawer chapel, with twelve foundation-stones on which are written the names of the twelve leading Congregationalists of Lupton, past the squalling children whose mothers were raiding and harrying them to bed. Then came the Free Library, an admirable instance, as the *Lupton Mercury* declared, of the adaptation of Gothic to modern requirements. From a sort of tower of this building a great arm shot out and hung a round clock-face over the street, and Meyrick experienced another shock when he saw that it was even later than he had feared. He had to get to the other side of the town, and it was past seven already! He began to run, wondering what his fate would be at his uncle’s hands, and he went by “our grand old parish church” (completely “restored” in the early ’forties), past the remains of the market-cross, converted most successfully, according to local opinion, into a drinking fountain for dogs and cattle, dodging his way among the late shoppers and the early loafers who lounged to and fro along the High Street.

===Chinese===
他终于望见最近的几条街上微弱的灯火——那是鞋匠们聚居的地方。他拖着沉重的步子匆匆穿过这片破落街区，走过一便士小铺，走过一家生涩的小酒馆、一座更加生涩的礼拜堂——堂前立着十二块奠基石，石上刻着卢普顿十二位公理会名流的姓名——又走过哭嚷叫闹的孩子们身边，他们的母亲正连吼带拽地把他们撵上床去。接着是免费图书馆，正如《卢普顿信使报》（*Lupton Mercury*）所宣称的，这是「哥特式建筑如何适应现代需要」的一个绝妙范例。楼上一座塔楼探出一支粗大的铁臂，把一面圆形钟盘悬在街道上空；梅里克一看，钟点竟比他担心还要晚，心里又是一惊。他必须赶到城的另一头，而七点钟已经敲过了！他撒腿跑起来，一面悬想舅舅会给他怎样的发落；他经过「我们宏伟古老的本堂教堂」（四十年代初被彻底「修复」过），经过市场十字架的残迹——照本地舆论的评断，它已被改造得极其成功，成了给狗和牛饮水的泉台——在散市晚归的购物人和早早出来闲荡的无事者中间左躲右闪，沿着大街穿行。

===Original===
He shuddered as he rang the bell at the Old Grange. He tried to put a bold face on it when the servant opened the door, and he would have gone straight down the hall into the schoolroom, but the girl stopped him.

“Master said you’re to go to the study at once, Master Meyrick, as soon as ever you come in.”

She was looking strangely at him, and the boy grew sick with dread. He was a “funk” through and through, and was frightened out of his wits about twelve times a day every day of his life. His uncle had said a few years before: “Lupton will make a man of you,” and Lupton was doing its best. The face of the miserable wretch whitened and grew wet; there was a choking sensation in his throat, and he felt very cold. Nelly Foran, the maid, still looked at him with strange, eager eyes, then whispered suddenly:

===Chinese===
他在老庄园（the Old Grange）拉响门铃时浑身一颤。女仆开门时，他竭力摆出一副满不在乎的神气，本想径直穿过门厅走进教室，却被那姑娘拦住了。

「主人说了，梅里克少爷，您一进门就得马上去书房。」

她直勾勾地望着他，眼神异样，那孩子吓得一阵阵发冷。他是个彻头彻尾的「胆小鬼（funk）」，一辈子天天里总有十二回上下吓得魂飞魄散。几年前舅舅说过：「卢普顿会把你造就成男子汉。」卢普顿正不遗余力地履行着这句话。这个可怜虫的脸白了，沁出汗来；喉咙里一阵哽噎，只觉遍体生寒。女仆内莉·福兰（Nelly Foran）仍用那种异样而热切的眼神望着他，忽然低声说：

===Original===
“You must go directly, Master Meyrick, Master heard the bell, I know; but I’ll make it up to you.”

Ambrose understood nothing except the approach of doom. He drew a long breath and knocked at the study door, and entered on his uncle’s command.

===Chinese===
「您得马上去，梅里克少爷，主人听见门铃了，我知道的；不过我会替您补上的。」

安布罗斯只听出末日将至，其余一概不懂。他深深吸了口气，敲响书房的门，奉命走了进去。

===Original===
It was an extremely comfortable room. The red curtains were drawn close, shutting out the dreary night, and there was a great fire of coal that bubbled unctuously and shot out great jets of flame﻿—in the schoolroom they used coke. The carpet was soft to the feet, and the chairs promised softness to the body, and the walls were well furnished with books. There were Thackeray, Dickens, Lord Lytton, uniform in red morocco, gilt extra; the Cambridge Bible for Students in many volumes, Stanley’s *Life of Arnold*, Coplestone’s *Praelectiones Academicae*, commentaries, dictionaries, first editions of Tennyson, school and college prizes in calf, and, of course, a great brigade of Latin and Greek classics. Three of the wonderful and terrible pictures of Piranesi hung in the room; these Mr. Horbury admired more for the subject-matter than for the treatment, in which he found, as he said, a certain lack of the *aurea mediocritas*﻿—almost, indeed, a touch of morbidity. The gas was turned low, for the High Usher was writing at his desk, and a shaded lamp cast a bright circle of light on a mass of papers.

===Chinese===
那是一间极其舒适的屋子。红色的窗帘拉得严严实实，把凄凉的夜色关在外面；一炉好煤烧得油润地咕嘟作响，蹿出大股大股的火苗——在教室里用的可是焦炭。地毯柔软宜足，椅子许诺着身躯的安适，四壁藏书也俨然可观：萨克雷、狄更斯、利顿勋爵，一律红色摩洛哥羊皮精装，烫金格外考究；多卷本的剑桥学生版《圣经》；斯坦利的《阿诺德传》（*Life of Arnold*）；科普尔斯通的《学院讲演录》（*Praelectiones Academicae*）；各类注释书、辞典；丁尼生的初版集子；小牛皮装帧的学校与大学奖品书；当然，还有浩浩荡荡一整队拉丁文与希腊文古典名著。屋里挂着三幅皮拉内西（Piranesi）那神奇而可怖的版画；霍伯里先生对它们的钦佩，更多在于题材，而非画法——照他的说法，那画法总嫌缺少一点「中庸之道」（*aurea mediocritas*），几乎可以说带几分病态。煤气灯拧得很低，因为高级学监（High Usher）正在书桌前写字；一盏带罩的灯在成堆的稿纸上投下一圈明亮的光。

===Original===
He turned round as Ambrose Meyrick came in. He had a high, bald forehead, and his fresh-coloured face was edged with reddish “mutton-chop” whiskers. There was a dangerous glint in his grey-green eyes, and his opening sentence was unpromising.

“Now, Ambrose, you must understand quite definitely that this sort of thing is not going to be tolerated any longer.”

===Chinese===
安布罗斯·梅里克进来时，他转过身来。他额头高而光秃，红润的面孔四周镶着一圈淡红色的连鬓「羊排须」。灰绿色的眼睛里闪着危险的光，开口第一句就不妙。

「听着，安布罗斯，你必须彻底明白：这种事情再也不能容忍了。」

===Original===
Perhaps it would not have fared quite so badly with the unhappy lad if only his uncle had not lunched with the Head. There was a concatenation accordingly, every link in which had helped to make Ambrose Meyrick’s position hopeless. In the first place there was boiled mutton for luncheon, and this was a dish hateful to Mr. Horbury’s palate. Secondly, the wine was sherry. Of this Mr. Horbury was very fond, but unfortunately the Head’s sherry, though making a specious appeal to the taste, was in reality far from good and teemed with those fiery and irritating spirits which make the liver to burn and rage. Then Chesson had practically found fault with his chief assistant’s work. He had not, of course, told him in so many words that he was unable to teach; he had merely remarked:

===Chinese===
这不幸的孩子本不至于落得这般惨——倘若舅舅没有同校长一起吃午饭的话。于是一连串因果接踵而至，每一环都把安布罗斯·梅里克的处境往绝路上推了一步。首先，午餐上了白煮羊肉，这道菜是霍伯里先生的口腹深恶痛绝的。其次，酒是雪利。霍伯里先生对雪利酒很有好感，可惜校长的雪利酒，虽然对味蕾摆出一副诱人的假面，实则绝非佳酿，而且满含那种烈性刺激的酒精，喝了叫人肝火烧灼、怒气翻腾。再者，切森博士实际上已对他的首席助手的功课挑了毛病。当然，他并没有明说自己不会教书；他只是说了这么一句：

===Original===
“I don’t know whether you’ve noticed it, Horbury, but it struck me the other day that there was a certain lack of grip about those fellows of yours in the fifth. Some of them struck me as *muddlers*, if you know what I mean: there was a sort of *vagueness*, for example, about their construing in that chorus. Have you remarked anything of the kind yourself?”

===Chinese===
「霍伯里，不知你注意到没有，前几天我总觉得，你五年级那班学生有点根基不牢。有些人给我的印象是*muddlers*（一团浆糊的角色），但愿我意思表达得清楚：比如说，他们解析那支合唱歌的时候，就有一种说不出的*vagueness*（含糊）。你自己看出这类情形了吗？」

===Original===
And then, again, the Head had gone on:

“And, by the way, Horbury, I don’t quite know what to make of your nephew, Meyrick. He was your wife’s nephew, wasn’t he? Yes. Well, I hardly know whether I can explain what I feel about the boy; but I can’t help saying that there is something wrong about him. His work strikes me as good enough﻿—in fact, quite above the form average﻿—but, to use the musical term, he seems to be in the wrong key. Of course, it may be my fancy; but the lad reminds me of those very objectionable persons who are said to have a joke up their sleeve. I doubt whether he is taking the Lupton stamp; and when he gets up in the school I shall be afraid of his influence on the other boys.”

===Chinese===
接着，校长又往下说：

「对了，霍伯里，你那个外甥梅里克，我总觉得拿他没办法。他是你太太的外甥，对吧？是的。唉，我对这孩子的观感，我几乎不知怎么说才好；但我不由得要说，他有些地方不对劲。他的功课在我看来够好的——实际上远在班上平均线之上——可是借用一个音乐术语，他似乎走错了调。当然，也许只是我的错觉；但这小子让我想起那种极讨人嫌的角色，人家说这种人袖子里总藏着一句俏皮话。我怀疑他能不能打上卢普顿的印记；等他在学校里升上高年级，我就要担心他对别的孩子施加的影响了。」

===Original===
Here, again, the master detected a note of blame; and by the time he reached the Old Grange he was in an evil humour. He hardly knew which he found the more offensive﻿—Chesson’s dish or his discourse. He was a dainty man in his feeding, and the thought of the great fat gigot pouring out a thin red stream from the gaping wound dealt to it by the Head mingled with his resentment of the indirect scolding which he considered that he had received, and on the fire just kindled every drop of that corrosive sherry was oil. He drank his tea in black silence, his rage growing fiercer for want of vent, and it is doubtful whether in his inmost heart he was altogether displeased when report was made at six o’clock that Meyrick had not come in. He saw a prospect﻿—more than a prospect﻿—of satisfactory relief.

===Chinese===
这话里又让这位教师听出了责难的音调；等到回到老庄园时，他已是一腔邪火。他简直说不清更叫他冒火的是切森的那道菜还是那番话。他是个讲究口腹的人；想到那根大肥羊腿被校长一刀砍开豁口、淌出细细一缕红肉汁的模样，又想到自己分明挨了一通指桑骂槐的训斥——两股怨气搅在一起；而那毒蚀肝肠的雪利酒，每一滴都浇在这把刚刚点燃的火上，成了浇油。他闷声不响、面色铁青地喝着茶，怒火因无处发泄而烧得愈旺。六点钟有人来报，说梅里克还没回来；他内心深处是否当真全然不悦，颇可怀疑。他看见了一种可意的解脱——不止是远景，而是近在眼前的解脱。

===Original===
Some philosophers have affirmed that lunatic doctors (or mental specialists) grow in time to a certain resemblance to their patients, or, in more direct language, become half mad themselves. There seems a good deal to be said for the position; indeed, it is probably a more noxious madness to swear a man into perpetual imprisonment in the company of maniacs and imbeciles because he sings in his bath and will wear a purple dressing-gown at dinner than to fancy oneself Emperor of China. However this may be, it is very certain that in many cases the schoolmaster is nothing more or less than a bloated schoolboy: the beasts are, radically, the same, but morbid conditions have increased the venom of the former’s sting. Indeed, it is not uncommon for well-wishers to the great Public School System to praise their favourite masters in terms which admit, nay, glory in, this identity. Read the memorial tributes to departed Heads in a well-known and most respectable Church paper. “To the last he was a big boy at heart,” writes Canon Diver of his friend, that illiterate old sycophant who brought up the numbers of the school to such a pitch by means of his conciliator policy to Jews, Turks, heretics and infidels that there was nothing for it but to make him a bishop. “I always thought he seemed more at home in the playing fields than in the sixth-form room.﻿ ﻿… He had all the English boy’s healthy horror of anything approaching pose or eccentricity.﻿ ﻿… He could be a severe disciplinarian when severity seemed necessary, but everybody in the school knew that a well-placed ‘boundary,’ a difficult catch or a goal well won or well averted would atone for all but the most serious offences.” There are many other points of resemblance between the average master and the average boy: each, for example, is intensely cruel, and experiences a quite abnormal joy in the infliction of pain. The baser boy tortures those animals which are not *méchants*. Tales have been told (they are hushed up by all true friends of the “System”) of wonderful and exquisite orgies in lonely hollows of the moors, in obscure and hidden thickets: tales of a boy or two, a lizard or a toad, and the slow simmering heat of a bonfire. But these are the exceptional pleasures of the virtuosi; for the average lad there is plenty of fun to be got out of his feebler fellows, of whom there are generally a few even in the healthiest community. After all, the weakest must go to the wall, and if the bones of the weakest are ground in the process, that is their fault. When some miserable little wretch, after a year or two of prolonged and exquisite torture of body and mind, seeks the last escape of suicide, one knows how the Old Boys will come forward, how gallantly they will declare that the days at the “dear old school” were the happiest in their lives; how “the Doctor” was their father and the Sixth their nursing-mother; how the delights of the Mahomedans’ fabled Paradise are but grey and weary sport compared with the joys of the happy fag, whose heart, as the inspired bard of Harrow tells us, will thrill in future years at the thought of the Hill. They write from all quarters, these brave Old Boys: from the hard-won Deanery, result of many years of indefatigable attack on the fundamental doctrines of the Christian faith; from the comfortable villa, the reward of commercial activity and acuteness on the Stock Exchange; from the courts and from the camps; from all the high seats of the successful; and common to them all is the convincing argument of praise. And we all agree, and say there is nothing like our great Public Schools, and perhaps the only dissentient voices are those of the father and mother who bury the body of a little child about whose neck is the black sign of the rope. But let them be comforted: the boy was no good at games, though his torments were not bad sport while he lasted.

===Chinese===
有些哲人断言，疯人院的医生（或精神病专家）日久天长会渐渐长得像他们的病人；说得直白些，就是自己也半疯了。这番主张似乎大有道理；说实在的，一个人因为在浴缸里唱歌、在餐桌上偏要穿紫色晨衣，就被人们赌咒发誓地判处终身监禁，与疯子和痴愚之徒为伴——这比起妄自尊大为中国的皇帝来，只怕是更毒的一种疯病。姑且不论孰是孰非，有一点却千真万确：许多情形下，所谓的学校教师（schoolmaster）说穿了不过是个吹胀了的大号学童（schoolboy）：这两种兽类根本上是一路货色，只是病态的境况使前一种刺上的毒液更烈。的确，那些热心拥护伟大公学体制的人，夸起他们心爱的教师来，言辞中竟公然承认、甚至引以为荣地炫示这种同一性，已是屡见不鲜。请读一读一家大名鼎鼎、极受敬重的教会报刊上悼念已故校长的文章吧。「直到最后，他骨子里始终是个大孩子，」戴弗教士（Canon Diver）这样写他的朋友——那个不学无术的老谄媚者，靠着他那套对犹太人、土耳其人、异端和不信者一律怀柔迁就的政策，把学校的人数抬到了那样的高峰，于是除了封他做主教，再没有别的出路。「我一向觉得，他在运动场上比在六年级教室里自在得多。……凡是近乎装腔作势或乖僻出格的东西，他都有英国男孩那种健全的深恶痛绝。……必要时他也是一位严厉的惩戒者，但全校的人都知道，一记打得恰到好处的『边界四分』（boundary），一记难度极高的接杀（catch），或者一个拿得下、守得住的球门，几乎什么过错都能将功折罪，最严重的除外。」寻常的教师和寻常的学童之间，还有许多别的相似之处：比如，两者都刻毒得厉害，都以施加痛苦为一种极不正常的欢愉。较下作的男孩，专折磨那些算不上*méchants*（凶恶难缠）的动物。有些传闻（一切「体制」的忠友都把嘴封得严严的）讲过荒原上冷僻的洼地里、僻暗的灌木丛中那些奇妙而精微的狂欢：讲一两个男孩、一只蜥蜴或一只癞蛤蟆，和一堆篝火慢慢熬煎的火候。不过这些是此道高手的特殊乐趣；寻常的孩子自有寻常的乐子——拿那些更懦弱的伙伴取乐，即使在最健康的群体里，也总有几个这样的活宝。归根结底，最弱者总要被逼到墙角；倘若最弱者的骨头在这过程中被碾碎，那是他们自己的错。当某个悲惨的小可怜虫，在一两年旷日持久、精雕细琢的身心折磨之后，寻求自杀这最后一道逃生口时，人们知道老校友们会怎样踊跃现身，怎样慷慨陈词，说「亲爱的老母校」的日子是他们一生中最幸福的时光；说那位「博士」（the Doctor）是他们的严父，六年级是他们的乳母；说穆斯林传说中那座乐园的一切欢愉，比起快活的「小听差」（fag）的快乐来，不过是灰色而倦怠的游戏——正如哈罗公学那位灵感充沛的诗人告诉我们的，这小听差的心，在往后的岁月里，一想起「哈罗山」（the Hill）就会激动震颤。四面八方的信件雪片般飞来，这些英勇的老校友：有的来自苦苦挣来的教长职位——那是多年不懈攻讦基督教基本教义的成果；有的来自舒适的别墅——那是证券交易场上商业干练与精明的报酬；有的来自法庭，有的来自军营；来自一切成功者的高位。而他们共同的雄辩，就是那篇赞美辞。于是我们全都点头称是，说什么也比不上我们伟大的公学；或许唯一的异议之声，来自那对下葬一个小小的孩子的父母——那孩子的脖颈上还留着黑黑的绳痕。但请二老节哀：这孩子在运动上是个废物；再说了，他活着的时候，他受的那些折磨也算不坏的消遣。

===Original===
Mr. Horbury was an old Luptonian; he was, in the words of Canon Diver, but “a big boy at heart,” and so he gave orders that Meyrick was to be sent in the study directly he came in, and he looked at the clock on the desk before him with satisfaction and yet with impatience. A hungry man may long for his delayed dinner almost with a sense of fury, and yet at the back of his mind he cannot help being consoled by the thought of how wonderfully he will enjoy the soup when it appears at last. When seven struck, Mr. Horbury moistened his lips slightly. He got up and felt cautiously behind one of the bookshelves. The object was there, and he sat down again. He listened; there were footfalls on the drive. Ah! there was the expected ring. There was a brief interval, and then a knock. The fire was glowing with red flashes, and the wretched toad was secured.

===Chinese===
霍伯里先生是卢普顿的老校友；用戴弗教士的话说，他也不过「骨子里是个大孩子」。所以他传下话来：梅里克一进门就立即送到书房；他望着书桌前方的挂钟，心满意足，却又焦灼难耐。一个饿汉等误了钟点的晚餐，几乎会等得怒火中烧，可心底深处却禁不住暗自宽慰，想着汤端上来时该是怎样的美味。七点的钟声敲响，霍伯里先生微微润了润嘴唇。他起身走到一个书架后面，小心翼翼地摸索了一下。那物件还在。他又坐了回去。他侧耳细听，车道上传来脚步声。啊！正是那声期待的铃响。短短一阵间隔，随后是敲门声。炉火正红光闪跳，那只可憎的癞蛤蟆落网了。

===Original===
“Now, Ambrose, you must understand quite definitely that this sort of thing isn’t going to be tolerated any longer. This is the third time during this term that you have been late for lockup. You know the rules: six o’clock at latest. It is now twenty minutes past seven. What excuse have you to make? What have you been doing with yourself? Have you been in the Fields?”

“No, Sir.”

“Why not? You must have seen the Resolution of the Sixth on the notice-board of the High School? You know what it promised any boy who shirked rocker? ‘A good sound thrashing with tuds before the First Thirty.’ I am afraid you will have a very bad time of it on Monday, after Graham has sent up your name to the Room.”

===Chinese===
「听着，安布罗斯，你必须彻底明白：这种事情再也不能容忍。这是本学期你第三次锁门点名迟到。规矩你是知道的：最迟六点。现在是七点二十分。你有什么可辩解的？这一向你都在干什么？你上操场去了吗？」

「没有，先生。」

「为什么不去？公学（High School）的布告栏上贴着六年级的决议，你不会没看见吧？你该知道它许诺给逃避 rocker（晃人游戏）的男孩什么待遇：『拉到 First Thirty（第一班级）面前，用 tuds（藤棍）结结实实痛打一顿。』恐怕到了星期一，等格雷厄姆（Graham）把你的名字报到 the Room（校长惩戒室）以后，你有得是苦头吃。」

===Original===
There was a pause. Mr. Horbury looked quietly and lengthily at the boy, who stood white and sick before him. He was a rather sallow, ugly lad of fifteen. There was something of intelligence in his expression, and it was this glance that Chesson, the Headmaster, had resented. His heart beat against his breast, his breath came in gasps and the sweat of terror poured down his body. The master gazed at him, and at last spoke again.

“But what have you been doing? Where have you been all this time?”

“If you please, Sir, I walked over to Selden Abbey.”

“To Selden Abbey? Why, it’s at least six miles away! What on earth did you want to go to Selden Abbey for? Are you fond of old stones?”

“If you please, Sir, I wanted to see the Norman arches. There is a picture of them in *Parker’s Glossary*.”

===Chinese===
有一阵停顿。霍伯里先生不动声色地久久打量着那孩子；孩子面色惨白、浑身难受地站在他面前。这是个面皮发黄、相貌丑陋的十五岁少年。他神情中有一种聪明的光芒，而校长切森博士所忌恨的，正是这道目光。他的心抵着胸口狂跳，呼吸一阵阵抽噎，恐怖的冷汗浇下身来。教师盯着他，终于又开口了。

「可你到底在干什么？这么半天你上哪儿去了？」

「先生，我走到塞尔登修道院（Selden Abbey）去了。」

「去塞尔登修道院？哎呀，那至少有六英里远！你究竟去塞尔登修道院干什么？你喜欢老石头吗？」

「先生，我想看看诺曼式拱券。《帕克建筑词汇》（*Parker’s Glossary*）里有一张它们的图。」

===Original===
“Oh, I see! You are a budding antiquarian, are you, Ambrose, with an interest in Norman arches﻿—eh? I suppose we are to look forward to the time when your researches will have made Lupton famous? Perhaps you would like to lecture to the school on St. Paul’s Cathedral? Pray, what are your views as to the age of Stonehenge?”

The wit was heavy enough, but the speaker’s position gave a bitter sting to his lash. Mr. Horbury saw that every cut had told, and, without prejudice to more immediate and acuter pleasures, he resolved that such biting satire must have a larger audience. Indeed, it was a long time before Ambrose Meyrick heard the last of those wretched Norman arches. The method was absurdly easy. “Openings” presented themselves every day. For example, if the boy made a mistake in construing, the retort was obvious:

“Thank you, Meyrick, for your most original ideas on the force of the aorist. Perhaps if you studied your Greek Grammar a little more and your favourite *Glossary of Architecture* a little less, it would be the better. Write out ‘Aorist means indefinite’ five hundred times.”

===Chinese===
「哦，原来如此！你是个初露头角的考古家了，是不是，安布罗斯，居然对诺曼拱券发生了兴趣——嗯？想必我们都在翘首以待，盼着你的研究让卢普顿名扬四海？或许你还乐意向全校师生开一场圣保罗大教堂的讲座？敢问阁下对巨石阵的年代有何高见？」

这俏皮话笨重得可以，但说话人的地位让他的鞭梢带着苦涩的倒钩。霍伯里先生看出每一下都抽得实在；于是，虽不耽误眼前更切身、更尖利的乐子，他打定主意，这般辛辣的挖苦必须推给更多的听众。的确，安布罗斯·梅里克听了那倒霉的诺曼拱券的余音，听了许久许久。办法荒谬地简单：「可乘之机」天天都有。譬如，那孩子解析出了错，反击便是现成的：

「多谢你，梅里克，你对不定过去式（aorist）用法的见解真是独到极了。倘若你多下点功夫研读希腊文法，少看点你心爱的那本《建筑词汇》，那对你的进益会更好些。把『不定过去式意为不定的』抄写五百遍。」

===Original===
Or, again, perhaps the Classic Orders were referred to. Mr. Horbury would begin to instruct the form as to the difference between Ionic and Doric. The form listened with poor imitation of interest. Suddenly the master would break off:

“I beg your pardon. I was forgetting that we have a great architectural authority amongst us. Be so kind as to instruct us, Meyrick. What does Parker say? Or perhaps you have excogitated some theories of your own? I know you have an original mind, from the extraordinary quantities of your last copy of verse. By the way, I must ask you to write out ‘The *e* in *venio* is short’ five hundred times. I am sorry to interfere with your more important architectural studies, but I am afraid there is no help for it.”

And so on; while the form howled with amusement.

===Chinese===
或者，或许又提起了古典柱式。霍伯里先生开始给全班讲授爱奥尼亚柱式与多立克柱式的分别。全班做出一副拙劣模仿的兴致在听。教师会突然打住：

「对不住，我竟忘了我们中间有一位建筑学的泰斗。梅里克，费心给我们开导开导吧。帕克是怎么说的？或者你已自行悟出了一套理论？我知道你心思独具一格——从你上次那篇韵文的离奇长短音里就看得出来。顺带说一句，我必须请你把『*venio* 中的 *e* 是短音』抄写五百遍。打断你更重要的建筑学研究，实在抱歉，可是恐怕没有别的办法。」

如此等等；全班则哄笑不止。

===Original===
But Mr. Horbury kept these gems for future and public use. For the moment he had more exciting work on hand. He burst out suddenly:

“The fact is, Ambrose Meyrick, you’re a miserable little humbug! You haven’t the honesty to say, fair and square, that you funked rocker and went loafing about the country, looking for any mischief you could lay your hands on. Instead of that you make up this cock-and-bull story of Selden Abbey and Norman arches﻿—as if any boy in his senses ever knew or cared twopence about such things! I hope you haven’t been spending the afternoon in some low public-house? There, don’t speak! I don’t want to hear any more lies. But, whatever you have been doing, you have broken the rules, and you must be taught that the rules have to be kept. Stand still!”

Mr. Horbury went to the bookshelf and drew out the object. He stood at a little distance behind Meyrick and opened proceedings with a savage cut at his right arm, well above the elbow. Then it was the turn of the left arm, and the master felt the cane bite so pleasantly into the flesh that he distributed some dozen cuts between the two arms. Then he turned his attention to the lad’s thighs and finished up in the orthodox manner, Meyrick bending over a chair.

===Chinese===
不过这些珠玑之言，霍伯里先生都留待日后公开使用。当下他手头有更刺激的营生。他突然爆发了：

「说白了，安布罗斯·梅里克，你就是个可怜的小骗子！你没胆量痛痛快快承认，你就是怕了 rocker，溜到乡下去闲逛，能碰上什么坏事就干什么坏事。反倒编出塞尔登修道院和诺曼拱券这套鬼话——好像哪个神志清醒的男孩听说过这种东西、肯为它费两便士的心思似的！但愿你这个下午没泡在哪家下等的小酒馆里？好了，别开口！你的谎话我不想再听了。可是，无论你干了什么，你违反了校规，就得叫你记住校规是不容违反的。站好别动！」

霍伯里先生走到书架前，把那物件抽了出来。他隔着几步站在梅里克身后，第一记便狠狠抽在他右臂上，高高在肘部以上，算是开了场。接着轮到左臂；藤条咬进肉里的滋味是那样可口，教师便在两条胳膊之间分发了十来下。随后他把注意力转到少年的大腿上，以正统的姿势收了尾——梅里克伏在椅背上。

===Original===
The boy’s whole body was one mass of burning, stinging torture; and, though he had not uttered a sound during the process, the tears were streaming down his cheeks. It was not the bodily anguish, though that was extreme enough, so much as a far-off recollection. He was quite a little boy, and his father, dead long since, was showing him the western doorway of a grey church on a high hill and carefully instructing him in the difference between “billetty” and “chevronny.”

“It’s no good snivelling, you know, Ambrose. I daresay you think me severe, but, though you won’t believe me now, the day will come when you will thank me from your heart for what I have just done. Let this day be a turning-point in your life. Now go to your work.”

===Chinese===
那孩子的全身成了一团火辣辣、刺戳戳的酷刑；尽管整个过程他没有出一声，泪水却顺着面颊直流。这与其说是肉体的剧痛——虽然那已痛到极处——不如说是一段遥远的回忆。他那时还是个小小的孩子，早已亡故的父亲正领他看一座高山之上灰石教堂的西门，仔细教他分辨纹章术语「billetty」（小方块纹）与「chevronny」（人字纹）的区别。

「抽抽搭搭没有用处，你知道，安布罗斯。我敢说你嫌我狠心；可是，尽管你现在不肯信，总有一天你会打心底里感激我方才的作为。让今天成为你一生的转折点吧。好了，做功课去。」

### II / II

===Original===
It was strange, but Meyrick never came in the after days and thanked his uncle for that sharp dose of physical and mental pain. Even when he was a man he dreamed of Mr. Horbury and woke up in a cold sweat, and then would fall asleep again with a great sigh of relief and gladness as he realised that he was no longer in the power of that “infernal old swine,” “that filthy, canting, cruel brute,” as he roughly called his old master.

The fact was, as some old Luptonians remarked, the two had never understood one another. With the majority of the boys the High Usher passed for a popular master enough. He had been a distinguished athlete in his time, and up to his last days at the school was a football enthusiast. Indeed, he organised a variety of the Lupton game which met with immense popularity till the Head was reluctantly compelled to stop it; some said because he always liked to drop bitter into Horbury’s cup when possible; others﻿—and with more probability on their side﻿—maintained that it was in consequence of a report received from the school doctor to the effect that this new species of football was rapidly setting up an old species of heart disease in the weaker players.

===Chinese===
说来奇怪，往后的岁月里，梅里克却从没有去谢过舅舅给他那剂身心剧痛的猛药。即使成年之后，他还会梦见霍伯里先生，从冷汗中惊醒；随即意识到自己已不再受那个「老鬼猪猡」、那个「满口假仁假义的下流畜生」——他这样粗声粗气地骂他的老校长——的摆布，才长长舒一口气，又安然睡去。

实情是——有几个老卢普顿人这样说过——这两个人从来就不曾互相了解。在大多数男孩眼中，这位高级学监算是相当受欢迎的教师。他当年是出类拔萃的运动健将，直到在校的最后岁月仍是足球的热心人。的确，他创制了卢普顿球戏的一个变种，大受欢迎，直到校长迫不得已把它禁了；有人说，那是因为校长一有机会总爱往霍伯里的杯子里滴几滴苦汁；另一些人——他们的说法恐怕更靠谱——则坚持说，那是由于校医呈交的一份报告，大意是这种新式足球正迅速让体弱的球员染上一种老式心脏病。

===Original===
However that might be, there could be no doubt as to Horbury’s intense and deep-rooted devotion to the school. His father had been a Luptonian before him. He himself had gone from the school to the University, and within a year or two of taking his degree he had returned to Lupton to serve it as a master. It was the general opinion in Public School circles that the High Usher had counted for as much as Chesson, the Headmaster, if not for more, in the immense advance in prestige and popularity that the school had made; and everybody thought that when Chesson received the episcopal order Horbury’s succession was a certainty. Unfortunately, however, there were wheels within wheels, and a total stranger was appointed, a man who knew nothing of the famous Lupton traditions, who (it was whispered) had been heard to say that “this athletic business” was getting a bit overdone. Mr. Horbury’s friends were furious, and Horbury himself, it was supposed, was bitterly disappointed. He retreated to one of the few decent canonries which have survived the wave of agricultural depression; but those who knew him best doubted whether his ecclesiastical duties were an adequate consolation for the loss of that coveted Headmastership of Lupton.

===Chinese===
不管真相如何，霍伯里对这学校那种热烈而深植的忠诚是无可置疑的。他的父亲比他更早是卢普顿人。他自己从本校升入大学，取得学位不过一两年，便回到卢普顿来，以教师之身效力母校。公学圈子里公认，在学校声望与名声的大进之中，这位高级学监的功劳纵使不比校长切森更大，至少也不相上下；人人都以为，切森一旦领受主教圣职，霍伯里的接任便是铁板钉钉。可惜内幕重重，最后任命了一位彻头彻尾的外人：此人全然不知卢普顿名扬遐迩的传统，而且（有人窃窃私语）曾听他亲口说过，「这股体育狂热」有点搞过头了。霍伯里的朋友们怒不可遏；霍伯里本人，据信也失望透顶。他退隐到农业萧条大潮之后硕果仅存的几处像样的教士俸职之一；不过最了解他的人都怀疑，他那点教会职务，能否补偿得回那顶人人觊觎的卢普顿校长之冠。

===Original===
To quote the memoir which appeared in the *Guardian* soon after his death, over some well-known initials:

“His friends were shocked when they saw him at the Residence. He seemed no longer the same man, he had aged more in six months, as some of them expressed themselves, than in the dozen years before. The old joyous Horbury, full of mirth, an apt master of wordplay and logic-fence, was somehow ‘dimmed,’ to use the happy phrase of a former colleague, the Dean of Dorchester. Old Boys who remembered the sparkle of his wit, the zest which he threw into everything, making the most ordinary form-work better fun than the games at other schools, as one of them observed, missed something indefinable from the man whom they had loved so long and so well. One of them, who had perhaps penetrated as closely as any into the arcana of Horbury’s friendship (a privilege which he will ever esteem as one of the greatest blessings of his life), tried to rouse him with an extravagant rumour which was then going the round of the popular Press, to the effect that considerable modifications were about to be introduced into the compulsory system of games at X., one of the greatest of our great Public Schools. Horbury flushed; the old light came into his eyes; his friend was reminded of the ancient warhorse who hears once more the inspiring notes of the trumpet. ‘I can’t believe it,’ he said, and there was a tremor in his voice. ‘They wouldn’t dare. Not even Y. (the Headmaster of X.) would do such a scoundrelly thing as that. I *won’t* believe it.’ But the flush soon faded and his apathy returned. ‘After all,’ he said, ‘I shouldn’t wonder if it were so. Our day is past, I suppose, and for all I know they may be construing the Breviary and playing dominoes at X. in a few years’ time.’”

===Chinese===
他死后不久，《卫报》（*the Guardian*，英国国教会周报）上刊出一篇悼文，署的是几个尽人皆知的首字母，其中说：

「在牧师公馆见到他的时候，他的朋友们都吓了一跳。他仿佛换了一个人；照他们中一些人的说法，六个月里苍老的程度，超过了此前整整十二年。昔日那个快活的霍伯里——满腹谐趣、妙语与论辩剑术的灵活高手——不知怎的『黯淡』了；这话是昔日的同僚、多尔切斯特教长（Dean of Dorchester）的一句妙语。老校友们记得他妙语的火花、他倾注在一切事物上的热情——照其中一位的说法，他把最寻常的班级功课都经营得比别校的游戏更好玩——如今他们从这位爱了这么久、爱得这么深的人身上，察觉到某种难以名状的东西不见了。其中一位，论窥探霍伯里友谊之堂奥，或许无人比他入得更深（这份荣幸，他将永远视作平生最大的福祉之一），想用当时小报间传得沸沸扬扬的一条耸人听闻的消息来振奋他：消息说，X.——我们伟大公学中最伟大者之一——即将对其强制性运动体制做大幅修改。霍伯里的脸红了；旧日的光彩回到他眼中；他的朋友不禁想起那匹重新听见嘹亮号角的老战马。『我不信，』他说，声音里带着颤抖。『他们不敢。连Y.（X.的校长）也干不出这种无耻勾当。我*绝不*信。』可是那阵红晕很快褪去，他又木然了。『话说回来，』他说，『真要那样我也不奇怪。我们的时代想必过去了；据我所知，再过几年，X.那儿的人没准儿念起日课经（Breviary）、玩起多米诺骨牌来了。』」

===Original===
“I am afraid that those last years at Wareham were far from happy. He felt, I think, out of tune with his surroundings, and, *pace* the readers of the *Guardian*, I doubt whether he was ever quite at home in his stall. He confessed to one of his old associates that he doubted the wisdom of the whole Cathedral system. ‘What,’ he said, in his old characteristic manner, ‘would St. Peter say if he could enter this building and see that gorgeous window in which he is represented with mitre, cope and keys?’ And I do not think that he was ever quite reconciled to the daily recitation of the Liturgy, accompanied as it is in such establishments by elaborate music and all the pomp of the surpliced choir. ‘Rome and water, Rome and water!’ he has been heard to mutter under his breath as the procession swept up the nave, and before he died I think that he had the satisfaction of feeling that many in high places were coming round to his views.

===Chinese===
「恐怕他在韦勒姆（Wareham）的最后那些年月远非幸福。我想，他总觉得自己与环境格格不入；恕《卫报》的读者们见怪（*pace*），我怀疑他在唱诗席上是否曾真正安顿过。他曾向一位旧日同侪承认，他对整座座堂体制的智慧心存怀疑。『假如圣彼得走进这座建筑，』他用他那副老派的做派说，『看见那扇华彩的彩窗——窗里他头戴冠冕、身披大氅、手执钥匙——他会说什么呢？』我也不认为他跟每日的礼拜经课真正和解过；在那种机构里，经课诵念还配有繁复的音乐和披白袍唱诗班的全部排场。迎宾行列扫过大殿之时，有人听见他低声嘀咕：『罗马掺水，罗马掺水！』而我想，在临终之前，他已颇感欣慰，觉得许多身居高位者正在转向他的见解。

===Original===
“But to the very last he never forgot Lupton. A year or two before he died he wrote the great school song, ‘Follow, follow, follow!’ He was pleased, I know, when it appeared in the *Luptonian*, and a famous Old Boy informs me that he will never forget Horbury’s delight when he was told that the song was already a great favourite in ‘Chantry.’ To many of your readers the words will be familiar; but I cannot resist quoting the first verse:

> “I am getting old and grey and the hills seem far away,
> And I cannot hear the horn that once proclaimed the morn
> When we sallied forth upon the chase together;
> For the years are gone﻿—alack!﻿—when we hastened on the track,
> And the huntsman’s whip went crack! as a signal to our pack
> Riding in the sunshine and fair weather.
> And yet across the ground
> I seem to hear a sound,
> A sound that comes up floating from the hollow;
> And its note is very clear
> As it echoes in my ear,
> And the words are: ‘Lupton, follow, follow, follow!’

===Chinese===
「可是直到最后一刻，他从没有忘记卢普顿。逝世前一两年，他写了那首伟大的校歌：『Follow, follow, follow!』（追随，追随，追随！）据我所知，这首歌在《卢普顿人》（*Luptonian*，母校刊物）上刊出时，他很觉欣然；还有一位声名显赫的老校友告诉我，当他听说这首歌在『Chantry』（校中一舍）已经大受欢迎时，霍伯里的那阵欢喜，他此生难忘。歌词对贵刊许多读者想必是熟悉的；可我忍不住要引它的第一节：

> 「我已经老了，鬓发霜白，群山显得那样遥远，
> 再也听不见那支号角——它曾宣告清晨来临，
> 那时我们一同纵马，奔赴围猎；
> 唉！那些岁月已逝——我们疾驰在猎径上，
> 猎手的鞭子啪啪作响，向我们的犬群发出号令，
> 在阳光与晴空里纵马飞驰。
> 然而越过原野，
> 我仿佛听见一个声音，
> 一个自幽谷飘然而上的声音；
> 它的调子十分清亮，
> 在我耳畔回荡，
> 那词句是：『卢普顿，追随，追随，追随！』」

===Original===
“An old pupil sang this verse to him on his deathbed, and I think, perhaps, that some at least of the readers of the *Guardian* will allow that George Horbury died ‘fortified,’ in the truest sense, ‘with the rites of the Church’﻿—the Church of a Great Aspiration.”

===Chinese===
「一位旧日的学生在他临终的榻前为他唱了这一节；我想，《卫报》的读者中至少总有一些人会承认：乔治·霍伯里（George Horbury）是在真正意义上『领受教会的圣礼』而『得以坚固』（fortified）地逝去的——在一个大有『雄心』的教会里。」

===Original===
Such was the impression that Mr. Horbury had evidently made upon some of his oldest friends; but Meyrick was, to the last, an infidel. He read the verses in the *Guardian* (he would never subscribe to the *Luptonian*) and jeered savagely at the whole sentiment of the memoir, and at the poetry, too.

“Isn’t it incredible?” he would say. “Let’s allow that the main purpose of the great Public Schools is to breed brave average boobies by means of rocker, sticker and mucker and the rest of it. Still, they do acknowledge that they have a sort of *parergon*﻿—the teaching of two great literatures, two literatures that have moulded the whole of Western thought for more than two thousand years. And they pay an animal like this to teach these literatures﻿—a swine that has not enough literature of any kind in him to save the soul of a louse! Look at those verses! Why, a decent fourth form boy would be ashamed to put his name to them!”

===Chinese===
霍伯里先生在他最老的一班朋友心中留下的，显然便是这样一种印象；可梅里克至死都是个不信者。他读了《卫报》上的那些诗句（他从不肯掏钱订《卢普顿人》），把整篇悼文的情调狠狠讥笑一番，连那诗也一并讥笑。

「岂有此理，不是么？」他常常这样说。「就算我们承认，伟大公学的头等要务是靠 rocker、sticker（曲棍球）、mucker（游泳课）以及诸如此类的名堂繁殖出一批批英勇的平庸笨蛋来，可它们好歹还承认自己有一样副业（*parergon*）——讲授两大文学：两千多年间塑造了整个西方思想的那两部文学。可它们偏偏雇这样一头畜生来教这两部文学——一头连一星半点的文学都拿不出来的猪猡，拿它连一只虱子的灵魂都救不了！你看看那几行诗！啧，一个像样的四年级男孩都不好意思署上自己的名字！」

===Original===
He was foolish to talk in this fashion. People merely said that it was evident he was one of the failures of the great Public School system; and the song was much admired in the right circles. A very well-turned *idem Latine* appeared in the *Guardian* shortly after the publication of the memoir, and the initials at the foot of the version were recognised as those of a literary dean.

And on that autumn evening, far away in the ’seventies, Meyrick, the boy, left Mr. Horbury’s study in a white fury of grief and pain and rage. He would have murdered his master without the faintest compunction, nay, with huge delight. Psychologically, his frame of mind was quite interesting, though he was only a schoolboy who had just had a sound thrashing for breaking rules.

===Chinese===
他这样说话是愚蠢的。人们只说：显而易见，此人正是伟大公学体制的失败品之一；而那首歌在体面圈子里大受激赏。悼文刊出后不久，《卫报》上又登出一篇笔致极工的拉丁文同调仿作（*idem Latine*），篇末的首字母被认出属于某位以文学见长的教长。

而在七十年代那个遥远的秋夜，男孩梅里克从霍伯里先生的书房里走出来，胸中是一片惨白的悲恸、剧痛与狂怒的漩涡。他会毫无内疚、不，会怀着巨大的快感，把他的老师杀掉。从心理学的角度看，他此刻的心境相当有趣——虽然他不过是个刚刚因为犯规而结结实实挨了一顿痛打的学童。

===Original===
For the fact, of course, was that Horbury, the irritating influence of the Head’s conversation and sherry apart, was by no means a bad fellow. He was for the moment savagely cruel, but then, most men are apt to be savagely cruel when they suffer from an inflamed liver and offensive superiors, more especially when there is an inferior, warranted defenceless, in their power. But, in the main, Horbury was a very decent specimen of his class﻿—English schoolmaster﻿—and Meyrick would never allow that. In all his reasoning about schools and schoolmasters there was a fatal flaw﻿—he blamed both for not being what they never pretended to be. To use a figure that would have appealed to him, it was if one quarrelled with a plain, old-fashioned meetinghouse because it was not in the least like Lincoln Cathedral. A chimney may not be a decorative object, but then it does not profess to be a spire or a pinnacle far in the spiritual city.

===Chinese===
因为实情当然是——撇开校长那番谈话和那壶雪利酒的刺激不谈——霍伯里绝算不得坏人。他此刻凶残得厉害；可是话说回来，肝火正旺、上头又是可憎的上司时，大多数人都会凶残得厉害，尤其手边还有一个照章程注定手无寸铁、只能任人摆布的下级时。大体而论，霍伯里是他那一类人——英国公学教师——中相当体面的一个标本；这一点梅里克死也不肯承认。他关于学校与教师的一切推理里，藏着一个致命的破绽：他责怪这两者不曾成为它们从来不曾自命的东西。用一个他准会中意的比喻说：这好比跟一座朴素的老式礼拜堂吵嘴，只因它一点不像林肯大教堂。烟囱也许算不得装饰物，可它也从来不曾自称是精神之城里远处的一座尖塔或塔尖。

===Original===
But Meyrick was always scolding meetinghouses because they were not cathedrals. He has been heard to rave for hours against useful, unpretentious chimney-pots because they bore no resemblance to celestial spires. Somehow or other, possibly by inheritance, possibly by the influence of his father’s companionship, he had unconsciously acquired a theory of life which bore no relation whatever to the facts of it. The theory was manifest in his later years; but it must have been stubbornly, if vaguely, present in him all through his boyhood. Take, for instance, his comment on poor Canon Horbury’s verses. He judged those, as we have seen, by the rules of the fine art of literature, and found them rubbish. Yet any old Luptonian would have told him that to hear the whole six hundred boys join in the chorus, “Lupton, follow away!” was one of the great experiences of life; from which it appears that the song, whatever its demerits from a literary point of view, fully satisfied the purpose for which it was written. In other words, it was an excellent chimney, but Meyrick still persisted in his easy and futile task of proving that it was not a bit like a spire. Then, again, one finds a fallacy of still huger extent in that major premise of his: that the great Public Schools purpose to themselves as a secondary and minor object the imparting of the spirit and beauty of the Greek and Latin literatures. Now, it is very possible that at some distant period in the past this was an object, or even, perhaps, *the* object of the institutions in question. The Humanists, it may be conjectured, thought of school and University as places where Latin and Greek were to be learned, and to be learned with the object of enjoying the great thought and the great style of an antique world. One sees the spirit of this in Rabelais, for example. The Classics are a wonderful adventure; to learn to understand them is to be a spiritual Columbus, a discoverer of new seas and unknown continents, a drinker of new-old wine in a new-old land. To the student of those days a mysterious drowned Atlantis again rose splendid from the waves of the great deep. It was these things that Meyrick (unconsciously, doubtless) expected to find in his school life; it was for the absence of these things that he continued to scold the system in his later years; wherein, like Jim in *Huckleberry Finn*, he missed the point by a thousand miles.

===Chinese===
可梅里克总是骂礼拜堂不像大教堂。有人听他一连几个钟头痛骂那些实用而朴实的烟囱管帽，只因它们长得不像天上的尖塔。不知怎的——或许是出于血统的遗传，或许是受了父亲朝夕相伴的熏陶——他不知不觉获得了一套与人生实况全不相干的人生理论。这理论在他晚年显露得明白；但它必定早在他的少年时代就隐隐约约、却固执地盘踞在他心里。比如他对可怜的霍伯里教士（Canon Horbury）那几行诗的评语。我们已经看到，他是按文学这门精美艺术的法则去评判的，并断定那是垃圾。可任何一个老卢普顿人都会告诉他：听全体六百名男孩齐声高唱「卢普顿，追下去！」的副歌，乃是人生一大奇遇；由此看来，这首歌无论从文学观点看有多少毛病，却完全满足了它写作的目的。换句话说，它是一根出色的烟囱；可梅里克偏要坚持他那件轻而易举却徒劳无功的事业，证明它一点也不像尖塔。再者，他的大前提里还藏着一个更其巨大的谬误：伟大公学把传授希腊拉丁文学的精神与美，当作一个次要而细小的目标。诚然，在久远的过去，这也许是这些机构的目标之一，甚至或许正是它们的*头等*目标。可以设想，人文主义者们（the Humanists）把学校和大学看作学习拉丁文与希腊文的地方，学习是为了领略一个古老世界的伟大思想与伟大风格。这种精神，比如在拉伯雷（Rabelais）身上便可见到。古典学问是一场奇妙的冒险；学会读懂古典，就是做一个精神上的哥伦布，发现新的海洋与未知的大陆，在一片新而古老的土地上啜饮新而陈的酒。对那个时代的学生，一座神秘的沉没的亚特兰蒂斯（Atlantis）重新从深海波涛里壮丽地升起。梅里克（无疑是不自觉地）正是期待着在学校生活里找到这些东西；也正是为了这些东西的缺席，他到了晚年还在继续痛骂那套体制；就此而言，他像《哈克贝利·费恩历险记》（*Huckleberry Finn*）里的杰姆（Jim）一样，差之千里，没抓住要点。

===Original===
The Latin and Greek of modern instruction are, of course, most curious and interesting survivals; no longer taught with any view of enabling students to enjoy and understand either the thought or beauty of the originals; taught rather in such a manner as to nauseate the learner for the rest of his days with the very notion of these lessons. Still, the study of the Classics survives, a curious and elaborate ritual, from which all sense and spirit have departed. One has only to recollect the form master’s lessons in the *Odyssey* or the *Bacchae*, and then to view modern Freemasons celebrating the Mystic Death and Resurrection of Hiram Abiff; the analogy is complete, for neither the master nor the Masons have the remotest notion of what they are doing. Both persevere in strange and mysterious actions from inveterate conservatism.

Meyrick was a lover of antiquity and a special lover of survivals, but he could never see that the round of Greek syntax, and Latin prose, of Elegiacs and verbs in *μι*, with the mystery of the *Oratio obliqua* and the Optative, was one of the most strange and picturesque survivals of modern life. It is to be noted, by the way, that the very meaning of the word “scholar” has been radically changed. Thus a well-known authority points out that “Melancholy” Burton had no “scholarship” in the real sense of the word; he merely used his vast knowledge of ancient and modern literature to make one of the most entertaining and curious books that the world possesses. True “scholarship,” in the modern sense, is to be sought for not in the Jacobean translators of the Bible, but in the Victorian revisers. The former made the greatest of English books out of their Hebrew and Greek originals; but the latter understood the force of the aorist. It is curious to reflect that “scholar” once meant a man of literary taste and knowledge.

===Chinese===
现代教学中的拉丁文与希腊文，自然是最古怪、最有趣的孑遗：再也不打算让学生能够领略和理解原作的任何思想或美，而是用这样一套教法，叫学习者后半辈子只要一想起这些功课就倒尽胃口。尽管如此，古典研究依然存续下来，成了一整套精巧古怪的仪轨，其中一切意义与精神早已荡然无存。你只需想一想班级教师讲授《奥德赛》（*Odyssey*）或《酒神的伴侣》（*Bacchae*）的功课，再想一想现代共济会会员庆祝希兰·阿比夫（Hiram Abiff）神秘的死与复活；两相比照，如出一辙：因为无论是教师还是共济会员，对自己正在做的事情都全然懵懂。两者都凭着根深蒂固的守旧脾气，固执于种种奇怪而神秘的举动。

梅里克是个爱好古物的人，尤其爱好那些孑遗的旧俗；可他始终看不出，希腊句法、拉丁散文、哀歌体诗（Elegiacs）与以 *μι* 结尾的动词的循环节奏，连同间接引语（*Oratio obliqua*）与祈愿式（Optative）的奥秘，正是现代生活中最古怪、最富画趣的孑遗之一。顺带应当指出，「学者」（scholar）一词的本义已经发生了根本变化。例如某位权威指出，「忧郁的」伯顿（Burton）按这个词的真正意义谈不上有什么「学术」（scholarship）；他只是用他那古今文学的渊博知识，写出了世间最有趣、最奇特的书籍之一。真正意义上的「学术」，在现代，不必到雅各一世时代的《圣经》译者中间去找，而要到维多利亚时代的修订者中间去找：前者从希伯来和希腊原文铸出了英文书籍中最伟大的一部；后者却懂得不定过去式的用法。想想也有趣：「scholar」从前指的，原是一个有文学趣味和学识的人。

===Original===
Meyrick never mastered these distinctions, or, if he did so in later years, he never confessed to his enlightenment, but went on railing at the meetinghouse, which, he still maintained, *did* pretend to be a cathedral. He has been heard to wonder why a certain Dean, who had pointed out the vast improvements that had been effected by the Revisers, did not employ a few young art students from Kensington to correct the infamous drawing of the fourteenth-century glass in his cathedral. He was incorrigible; he was always incorrigible, and thus, in his boyhood, on the dark November evening, he meditated the murder of his good master and uncle﻿—for at least a quarter of an hour.

His father, he remembered, had always spoken of Gothic architecture as the most wonderful and beautiful thing in the world: a thing to be studied and loved and reverenced. His father had never so much as mentioned rocker, much less had he preached it as the one way by which an English boy must be saved. Hence, Ambrose maintained inwardly that his visit to Selden Abbey was deserving of reward rather than punishment, and he resented bitterly, the savage injustice (as he thought it) of his caning.

===Chinese===
梅里克从没弄通这些分别；或者说，即使他晚年弄通了，也从不肯承认自己开了窍，照旧咒骂那座礼拜堂，依然一口咬定它*分明*自命为大教堂。有人听他说过怪话：某位教长——他曾指出修订者们促成了何等巨大的改进——却为什么不用几个从肯辛顿（Kensington）来的年轻美术学生，去改掉他那座大教堂里十四世纪彩绘玻璃上恶劣的图样。他无可救药；他向来无可救药；于是，在少年时代那个黑暗的十一月夜晚，他盘算起谋杀他的好老师、好舅舅来——至少盘算了有一刻钟之久。

他记得父亲向来把哥特式建筑称作世上最奇妙、最美的事物：一件应当研习、热爱、崇敬的东西。父亲从没有提起过 rocker 一词，更不曾把它鼓吹成英国男孩得救的唯一途径。因此，安布罗斯内心始终认为，他去塞尔登修道院的那一趟，该受奖赏而不是受惩罚；那顿藤条的横蛮不公（在他看来如此），他怨毒在心。

### III / III

===Original===
Yet Mr. Horbury had been right in one matter, if not in all. That evening was a turning-point in Meyrick’s life. He had felt the utmost rage of the enemy, as it were, and he determined that he would be a funk no longer. He would not degenerate into the state of little Phipps, who had been bullied and “rockered” and beaten into such a deplorable condition that he fainted dead away while the Headmaster was operating on him for “systematic and deliberate lying.” Phipps not only fainted, but, being fundamentally sensible, as Dr. Johnson expressed it, showed a strong disinclination to return to consciousness and the precious balms of the “dear old Head.” Chesson was rather frightened, and the school doctor, who had his living to get, said, somewhat dryly, that he thought the lad had better go home for a week or two.

So Phipps went home in a state which made his mother cry bitterly and his father wonder whether the Public School system was not overpraised. But the old family doctor went about raging and swearing at the “scoundrels” who had reduced a child of twelve to a nervous wreck, with “neurasthenia cerebralis” well on its way. But Dr. Walford had got his education in some trumpery little academy, and did not understand or value the ethos of the great Public Schools.

===Chinese===
不过，即便霍伯里先生并非事事都对，他在一件事上却是对的。那天晚上是梅里克一生的转折点。他算是尝到了敌人的极怒，从此打定主意，再不做胆小鬼。他决不肯堕落到小菲普斯（Phipps）的田地——那孩子被欺凌、被「晃」（rockered）、被抽打，落到了那样可悲的地步，以至于校长正为他「系统性、蓄意说谎」行惩戒时，他竟直挺挺昏死了过去。菲普斯不止昏倒，而且——照约翰逊博士（Dr. Johnson）的说法，他骨子里原是个明白人——表现出强烈的意愿，不愿再回到清醒世界，回到「亲爱的老校长」的贵重香膏里来。切森吓得不轻；校医还得自己挣饭碗，便干巴巴地说，他看这孩子还是回家休养一两个星期为好。

于是菲普斯回了一趟家，那副模样叫母亲哭得死去活来，叫父亲疑心公学体制是不是被捧得太高了。可那位老家庭医生却四处奔走，破口大骂那些把一个十二岁的孩子弄成神经残废、眼看「脑神经衰弱」（*neurasthenia cerebralis*）就要缠身的「恶棍」。不过沃尔福德博士（Dr. Walford）是在某个寒碜的小学堂里受的教育，不懂也不看重伟大公学的精神气度。

===Original===
Now, Ambrose Meyrick had marked the career of wretched Phipps with concern and pity. The miserable little creature had been brought by careful handling from masters and boys to such a pitch of neurotic perfection that it was only necessary to tap him smartly on the back or on the arm, and he would instantly burst into tears. Whenever anyone asked him the simplest question he suspected a cruel trap of some sort, and lied and equivocated and shuffled with a pitiable lack of skill. Though he was pitched by the heels into mucker about three times a week, that he might acquire the useful art of natation, he still seemed to grow dirtier and dirtier. His school books were torn to bits, his exercises made into darts; he had impositions for losing books and canings for not doing his work, and he lied and cried all the more.

Meyrick had never got to this depth. He was a sturdy boy, and Phipps had always been a weakly little animal; but, as he walked from the study to the schoolroom after his thrashing, he felt that he had been in some danger of descending on that sad way. He finally resolved that he would never tread it, and so he walked past the baize-lined doors into the room where the other boys were at work on prep, with an air of unconcern which was not in the least assumed.

Mr. Horbury was a man of considerable private means and did not care to be bothered with the troubles and responsibilities of a big House. But there was room and to spare in the Old Grange, so he took three boys besides his nephew. These three were waiting with a grin of anticipation, since the nature of Meyrick’s interview with “old Horbury” was not dubious. But Ambrose strolled in with a “Hallo, you fellows!” and sat down in his place as if nothing had happened. This was intolerable.

===Chinese===
话说回来，安布罗斯·梅里克一直关切而怜悯地注视着可怜的菲普斯的经历。这个悲惨的小生灵，经教师与男孩们「悉心调理」，已被调教到神经质的极妙境界：只要在他背上或胳膊上轻轻一拍，他立时就会哭出声来。随便谁问他一个最简单的问题，他都疑心是某种狠毒的圈套，于是撒谎、支吾、躲闪，技巧拙劣得叫人可怜。尽管为了让他习得游泳这门有用的技艺，人们每周三次上下把他倒拎着脚扔进 mucker，他看上去还是越弄越脏。他的课本被撕成碎片，练习册被折成飞镖；丢了书要罚抄（imposition），不做功课要挨藤条，于是他越发又哭又谎。

梅里克从没有堕落到这般田地。他是个结实的孩子，菲普斯却素来是只孱弱的小动物；可是挨完打，从书房往教室走的时候，他觉得自己险些也踏上那条悲惨的路。他终于立誓绝不再走那条路；于是他走过一道道衬着厚呢的门，走进那间其他孩子正在做晚自习功课的屋子，一脸若无其事的神气——没有半点做作。

霍伯里先生私产颇丰，不愿意让一座大宿舍（House）的麻烦和责任来烦扰自己。可老庄园里屋宇宽绰有余，于是除了外甥之外，他又收留了三个男孩。这三个正咧着嘴、满心期待地等着看戏，因为梅里克同「老霍伯里」那场会面的性质是不言自明的。可是安布罗斯踱着步子进来，一句「喂，伙计们！」，便在自己座位上坐下了，仿佛什么都没发生过。这叫人忍无可忍。

===Original===
“I say, Meyrick,” began Pelly, a beefy boy with a red face, “you *have* been blubbing! Feel like writing home about it? Oh! I forgot. This is your home, isn’t it? How many cuts? I didn’t hear you howl.”

The boy took no notice. He was getting out his books as if no one had spoken.

“Can’t you answer?” went on the beefy one. “How many cuts, you young sneak?”

“Go to hell!”

The whole three stared aghast for a moment; they thought Meyrick must have gone mad. Only one, Bates the observant, began to chuckle quietly to himself, for he did not like Pelly. He who was always beefy became beefier; his eyes bulged out with fury.

===Chinese===
「我说，梅里克，」佩利（Pelly）开了腔，这是个红脸膛的粗壮孩子，「你*哭*过了！想不想写封家书哭诉一番？哦！我忘了。这儿就是你的家，不是么？挨了几藤条？我可没听见你嚎。」

那孩子毫不理会。他正掏出书本，仿佛没人说过话。

「哑巴了？」那个壮汉接着说。「挨了几藤条，你个小奸细？」

「见鬼去吧！」

三个人全都惊得呆了一瞬；他们以为梅里克准是疯了。只有一个——善于观察的贝茨（Bates）——独自暗暗吃吃笑了起来，因为他不喜欢佩利。本就粗壮的佩利愈发横粗起来；眼睛因狂怒暴凸出去。

===Original===
“I’ll give it you,” he said and made for Ambrose, who was turning over the leaves of the Latin dictionary. Ambrose did not wait for the assault; he rose also and met Pelly halfway with a furious blow, well planted on the nose. Pelly took a back somersault and fell with a crash to the floor, where he lay for a moment half stunned. He rose staggering and looked about him with a pathetic, bewildered air; for, indeed, a great part of his little world had crumbled about his ears. He stood in the middle of the room, wondering what it meant, whether it was true indeed that Meyrick was no longer of any use for a little quiet fun. A horrible and incredible transmutation had, apparently, been effected in the funk of old. Pelly gazed wildly about him as he tried to staunch the blood that poured over his mouth.

“Foul blow!” ventured Rawson, a lean lad who liked to twist the arms of very little boys till they shrieked for mercy. The full inwardness of the incident had not penetrated to his brain; he saw without believing, in the manner of the materialist who denies the marvellous even when it is before his eyes.

“Foul blow, young Meyrick!”

===Chinese===
「看我收拾你，」他说着便朝安布罗斯扑去，后者正在翻拉丁词典的书页。安布罗斯没有坐等袭击；他也站起身，在半道上迎住佩利，一拳结结实实地砸在他鼻子上。佩利倒翻了一个筋斗，轰然跌在地上，半晌昏迷不醒。他摇摇晃晃爬起来，用一种可怜而茫然的模样四下张望；因为，说实话，他的小世界里有一大块就在耳边崩塌了。他站在屋子中央，纳闷这一切是什么意思，梅里克当真再也不能拿来开心解闷了么——这是不是真的。显然，一场可怕而难以置信的蜕变，已在昔日那个胆小鬼身上完成。佩利疯狂地环顾四周，一面竭力捂住从嘴巴上涌流的血。

「使阴招！」罗森（Rawson）冒出一句，这是个瘦削的男孩，喜欢拧小小孩的胳膊，一直拧到他们尖叫讨饶。这场变故的全部内情还没有渗进他的脑子；他看见了却不肯相信，一副唯物主义者的做派——奇迹摆在眼前也死不认账。

「使阴招了，小梅里克！」

===Original===
The quiet student had gone back to his place and was again handling his dictionary. It was a hard, compact volume, rebound in strong boards, and the edge of these boards caught the unfortunate Rawson full across the eyes with extraordinary force. He put his face in his hands and blubbered quietly and dismally, rocking to and fro in his seat, hardly hearing the fluent stream of curses with which the quiet student inquired whether the blow he had just had was good enough for him.

Meyrick picked up his dictionary with a volley of remarks which would have done credit to an old-fashioned stage-manager at the last dress rehearsal before production.

“Hark at him,” said Pelly feebly, almost reverently. “Hark at him.” But poor Rawson, rocking to and fro, his head between his hands, went on blubbering softly and spoke no word.

===Chinese===
那安静的学生回到座位上，又摆弄起他的词典来。那是一部坚硬、厚实的书，用结实的硬板重新装订过；硬板的边棱以惊人的力道正正磕在不幸的罗森双眼上。他双手掩面，低低地、凄惨地抽泣起来，在座位上前仰后合地摇晃，几乎听不见那位安静学生滔滔不绝的咒骂——他在问方才那一下够不够他受用。

梅里克拾起词典，嘴里弹出一连串叫骂——那连珠炮足以为一个老派舞台监督在公演前最后一次彩排上增光添彩。

「听听他，」佩利有气无力地、几乎虔敬地说。「听听他。」可是可怜的罗森双手抱头、前后摇晃，只是低声抽泣，一言不发。

===Original===
Meyrick had never been an unobservant lad; he had simply made a discovery that evening that in Rome certain Roman customs must be adopted. The wise Bates went on doing his copy of Latin verse, chuckling gently to himself. Bates was a cynic. He despised all the customs and manners of the place most heartily and took the most curious care to observe them. He might have been the inventor and patentee of rocker, if one judged him by the fervour with which he played it. He entered his name for every possible event at the sports, and jumped the jumps and threw the hammer and ran the races as if his life depended on it. Once Mr. Horbury had accidentally overheard Bates saying something about “the honour of the House” which went to his heart. As for cricket, Bates played as if his sole ambition was to become a first-class professional. And he chuckled as he did his Latin verses, which he wrote (to the awe of other boys) “as if he were writing a letter”﻿—that is, without making a rough copy. For Bates had got the “hang” of the whole system from rocker to Latin verse, and his copies were much admired. He grinned that evening, partly at the transmutation of Meyrick and partly at the line he was jotting down:

> “Mira loquor, cœlo resonans vox funditur alto.”

===Chinese===
梅里克从来不是个不长眼的孩子；那晚他只是悟出一个道理：在罗马，有些罗马规矩也须照办。睿智的贝茨继续誊他的拉丁诗，独自轻轻吃吃地笑。贝茨是个愤世者。他把此地的一切规矩习俗从心底里鄙视个透，却又以最古怪的谨慎小心一一遵行。若单凭他玩 rocker 的那股狂热来判断，他简直可以算是 rocker 的发明人和专利持有人。运动会上凡有项目他必定报名，跳的跳、掷链锤的掷链锤、赛跑的赛跑，好像性命都系在那上头。有一回霍伯里先生无意中听见贝茨说起什么「为宿舍争光」，那话说到他心坎里去了。至于板球，贝茨打起来仿佛他唯一的抱负就是成为一名一流的职业球员。他一面做拉丁诗一面吃吃地笑；他的拉丁诗（别的男孩看得啧啧称奇）「写得像写家信」一样——意思是，不打草稿。因为贝茨已把从 rocker 到拉丁诗的整章体制的门道全部摸透，他的誊作深受称赏。那天晚上他咧嘴而笑，一半笑梅里克的蜕变，一半笑他正在写下的那一行：

> “Mira loquor, cœlo resonans vox funditur alto.”（「我述说奇事，清越的声响自高天倾泻而下。」）

===Original===
In after life he jotted down a couple of novels which sold, as the journalists said, “like hot cakes.” Meyrick went to see him soon after the first novel had gone into its thirtieth thousand, and Bates was reading “appreciations” and fingering a cheque and chuckling.

“*Mira loquor, populo, resonans*, cheque *funditur alto*,” he said. “I know what schoolmasters and boys and the public want, and I take care they get it﻿—*sale espèce de sacrés cochons de N. de D.*!”

The rest of prep went off quite quietly. Pelly was slowly recovering from the shock that he had received and began to meditate revenge. Meyrick had got him unawares, he reflected. It was merely an accident, and he resolved to challenge Meyrick to fight and give him back the worst licking he had ever had in his life. He was beefy, but a bold fellow. Rawson, who was really a cruel coward and a sneak, had made up his mind that he wanted no more, and from time to time cast meek and propitiatory glances in Meyrick’s direction.

===Chinese===
后来他随手炮制了两部小说，销量如报人们所说，「像热饼一样」疯卖。第一部小说刚销过三万册（第三十版次），梅里克就去拜访他；贝茨正在读「赞誉文章」，捻着一张支票，咯咯直笑。

「*Mira loquor, populo, resonans*, cheque *funditur alto*（『我向世人述说奇事，支票自高天倾落』——把原句的*cœlo*〔高天〕换成了*populo*〔世人〕，*vox*〔声音〕换成了支票），」他说。「我深知教师、学生和公众想要什么，也一定让他们如愿——*sale espèce de sacrés cochons de N. de D.*（下流的、拿神名赌咒的圣猪们）！」

晚自习剩下的时光安安静静地过去了。佩利渐渐从震惊里缓过劲来，开始盘算复仇。他思忖：梅里克是趁其不备得了手；那不过是场意外；他决意约梅里克正式打一架，把他此生挨过的最惨的一顿奉还给他。他虽粗壮，却是个胆大的家伙。罗森——其实是个残忍的懦夫兼告密胚——早已拿定主意再也不要了，还不时朝梅里克的方向怯生生地投去讨好求和的眼风。

===Original===
At half-past nine they all went into their dining-room for bread and cheese and beer. At a quarter to ten Mr. Horbury appeared in cap and gown and read a chapter from St. Paul’s Epistle to the Romans, with one or two singularly maundering and unhappy prayers. He stopped the boys as they were going up to their rooms.

“What’s this, Pelly?” he said. “Your nose is all swollen. It’s been bleeding, too, I see. What have you been doing to yourself? And you, Rawson, how do you account for your eyes being black? What’s the meaning of all this?”

===Chinese===
九点半，他们一齐到膳厅去吃面包、奶酪，喝啤酒。十点差一刻，霍伯里先生戴着方帽、披着长袍出现，诵读《圣保罗致罗马人书》中的一章，外加一两篇异样絮叨、愁眉苦脸的祷文。孩子们正要上楼回房，被他叫住了。

「这是怎么了，佩利？」他说。「你鼻子全肿了。我也看得出，它还流过血。你把自己怎么了？还有你，罗森，你两个眼睛发青，怎么交代？这都算怎么回事？」

===Original===
“Please, Sir, there was a very stiff bully down at rocker this afternoon, and Rawson and I got tokered badly.”

“Were you in the bully, Bates?”

“No, Sir; I’ve been outside since the beginning of the term. But all the fellows were playing up tremendously, and I saw Rawson and Pelly had been touched when we were changing.”

“Ah! I see. I’m very glad to find the House plays up so well. As for you, Bates, I hear you’re the best outside for your age that we’ve ever had. Good night.”

The three said “Thank you, Sir,” as if their dearest wish had been gratified, and the master could have sworn that Bates flushed with pleasure at his word of praise. But the fact was that Bates had “suggested” the flush by a cunning arrangement of his features.

===Chinese===
「先生，今天下午 rocker 场上有过一场非常激烈的 bully（争球混战），我和罗森被 tokered（狠揍）得很厉害。」

「争球的时候你在场么，贝茨？」

「不在，先生；我从学期开始就在打外线。不过大家伙儿都拼命为舍争光，我们换衣服的时候，我就看见罗森和佩利挂了彩。」

「啊！明白了。宿舍的人这样为舍争光，我很高兴。至于你，贝茨，我听说，按你的年纪说，你是我们历届中最好的外线。晚安。」

三个人齐声道了声「谢谢您，先生」，仿佛此生最大的心愿得到了满足；那位教师若赌咒，定会说贝茨听见这句夸奖高兴得满脸通红。可实情是，那阵红晕是贝茨靠五官的巧妙调度「演示」出来的。

===Original===
The boys vanished and Mr. Horbury returned to his desk. He was editing a selection called “English Literature for Lower Forms.” He began to read from the slips that he had prepared:

> “So all day long the noise of battle roll’d
> Among the mountains by the winter sea;
> Until King Arthur’s table, man by man,
> Had fallen in Lyonnesse﻿—”

He stopped and set a figure by the last word, and then, on a blank slip, with a corresponding letter, he repeated the figure and wrote the note:

> Lyonnesse=the Scilly Isles.

Then he took a third slip and wrote the question:

> Give the ancient name of the Scilly Isles.

===Chinese===
孩子们散去了，霍伯里先生回到书桌前。他正在编一部选集，题为《低年级英国文学选》（*English Literature for Lower Forms*）。他开始诵读已备好的活页纸条：

> 「一整天，战斗的喧嚣滚滚而过，
> 冬海之滨的群山之间；
> 直到亚瑟王的圆桌，一个接一个，
> 在里昂尼斯全部倒下——」

他停住，在最后一个词旁标了一个数码；随后在一张空白活页上写上相应的字母，重标那个数码，写下注语：

> Lyonnesse＝锡利群岛（the Scilly Isles）。

然后他取了第三张活页，写下问题：

> 写出锡利群岛的古名。

===Original===
These serious labours employed him till twelve o’clock. He put the materials of his book away as the clock struck, and solemnly mixed himself his nightly glass of whisky and soda﻿—in the daytime he never touched spirits﻿—and bit the one cigar which he smoked in the twenty-four hours. The stings of the Head’s sherry and of his conversation no longer burned within him; time and work and the bite of the cane in Meyrick’s flesh had soothed his soul, and he set himself to dream, leaning back in his armchair, watching the cheerful fire.

He was thinking of what he would do when he succeeded to the Headmastership. Already there were rumours that Chesson had refused the Bishopric of St. Dubric’s in order that he might be free to accept Dorchester, which, in the nature of things, must soon be vacant. Horbury had no doubt that the Headmastership would be his; he had influential friends who assured him that the trustees would not hesitate for an instant. Then he would show the world what an English Public School could be made. In five years, he calculated, he would double the numbers. He saw the coming importance of the modern side, and especially of science. Personally, he detested “stinks,” but he knew what an effect he would produce with a great laboratory fitted with the very best appliances and directed by a highly qualified master. Then, again, an elaborate gymnasium must be built; there must be an engineer’s shop, too, and a carpenter’s as well. And people were beginning to complain that a Public School Education was of no use in the City. There must be a business master, an expert from the Stock Exchange who would see that this reproach was removed. Then he considered that a large number of the boys belonged to the land-owning class. Why should a country gentleman be at the mercy of his agent, forced for lack of technical knowledge to accept statements which he could not check? It was clear that the management of land and great estates must have its part in the scheme; and, again, the best-known of the Crammers must be bought on his own terms, so that the boys who wished to get into the Army or the Civil Service would be practically compelled to come to Lupton. Already he saw paragraphs in the *Guardian* and *The Times*﻿—in all the papers﻿—paragraphs which mentioned the fact that ninety-five percent of the successful candidates for the Indian Civil Service had received their education at the foundation of “stout old Martin Rolle.” Meanwhile, in all this flood of novelty, the old traditions should be maintained with more vigour than ever. The classics should be taught as they never had been taught. Every one of the masters on this side should be in the highest honours and, if possible, he would get famous men for the work﻿—they should not merely be good, but also notorious scholars. Gee, the famous explorer in Crete, who had made an enormous mark in regions widely removed from the scholastic world by his wonderful book, *Daedalus; or, the Secret of the Labyrinth*, must come to Lupton at any price; and Maynard, who had discovered some most important Greek manuscripts in Egypt, he must have a form, too. Then there was Rendell, who had done so well with his *Thucydides*, and Davies, author of *The Olive of Athene*, a daring but most brilliant book which promised to upset the whole established theory of mythology﻿—he would have such a staff as no school had ever dreamed of. “We shall have no difficulty about paying them,” thought Horbury; “our numbers will go up by leaps and bounds, and the fees shall be five hundred pounds a year﻿—and such terms will do us more good than anything.”

===Chinese===
这些庄重的劳作让他一直忙到十二点。钟声敲响时，他把书稿材料收拾起来，郑重其事地给自己调了每晚一杯的威士忌苏打——白天他从不动酒——又咬开他一天里唯一的一支雪茄。校长的雪利酒和校长那番话的蜇刺，已不再在他体内燃烧；时光、劳作，还有藤条咬进梅里克皮肉的滋味，抚慰了他的灵魂；他仰靠进扶手椅，凝望着欢快的炉火，做起梦来。

他在想，等自己接任校长之后要做的事。已经有传言说，切森辞退了圣杜比修（St. Dubric’s）的主教之职，为的是腾出身来接受多尔切斯特的职位——那职位依常理必然不久出缺。霍伯里毫不怀疑校长之位终归是他的；他有的是有势力的朋友，这些人向他担保，托管人不会有片刻迟疑。到那时，他要让全世界看看，一所英国公学能办成什么样子。据他估算，五年之内，学生人数要翻一番。他看出现代科（modern side）的前程，尤其是科学的分量。他本人厌恶「stinks」（「臭气」，校园里对化学的谑称），但他明白，一座设备极其精良、由一位资历极高的大师主持的大实验室，会造成怎样的轰动。再者，还必须建一座精良的健身房；得有一间工程师工场，还得有一间木工场。人们已经开始抱怨公学教育在伦敦城里毫无用处。必须设一位商科教师——一位证券交易所的行家，保证洗刷这一耻辱。他又想到，学生里颇有几个出身有地的阶层。乡绅凭什么要听凭经纪人摆布，只因缺少专业知识，被迫接受自己无从核对的账目？不言而喻，土地与大宗产业的经营必须在方案里占一席之地；还有，最负盛名的那几家补习学塾（Crammers）必须按他开的条件收买下来，好让想进军队或文官系统的学生实际上不得不投到卢普顿门下。他已经在《卫报》和《泰晤士报》——在所有报纸上——看见那样的段落了：段落里提到，印度文官考试的成功考生，百分之九十五都出自『硬朗的老马丁·罗尔（Martin Rolle）』创办的这所学校。与此同时，在这股新潮流的洪水之中，旧传统要加倍有力地维持。古典功课要教得前所未有地好。这一路的每位教师都得是一等名誉学位得主；可能的话，他要延聘名家来执教——他们不光要优秀，还得是有名的学者。吉（Gee），克里特岛上那位著名的探险家，凭他那本奇书《代达罗斯；或，迷宫之谜》（*Daedalus; or, the Secret of the Labyrinth*）在远离教育界的领域里留下了巨大的印记，无论如何代价也要请到卢普顿来；还有梅纳德（Maynard），在埃及发现过几种极其重要的希腊抄本，也得给他一个班。还有伦德尔（Rendell），他那部《修昔底德》做得如此出色；还有戴维斯（Davies），《雅典娜的橄榄》（*The Olive of Athene*）的作者——一部大胆却才华横溢的书，预示着要把整个公认的神话说掀翻——他要有一支任何学校做梦也想不到的教师队伍。『给他们发薪水不成问题，』霍伯里想，『我们的人数会突飞猛涨，学费定为一年五百镑——这样的条件比什么广告都管用。』

===Original===
He went into minute detail. He must take expert advice as to the advisability of the school farming on its own account, and so supplying the boys with meat, milk, bread, butter and vegetables at first cost. He believed it could be done; he would get a Scotch farmer from the Lowlands and make him superintendent at a handsome salary and with a share in the profits. There would be the splendid advertisement of “the whole dietary of the school supplied from the School Farms, under the supervision of Mr. David Anderson, formerly of Haddanneuk, the largest tenancy in the Duke of Ayr’s estates.” The food would be better and cheaper, too; but there would be no luxury. The “Spartan” card was always worth playing; one must strike the note of plain living in a luxurious age; there must be no losing of the old Public School severity. On the other hand, the boy’s hands should be free to go into their own pockets; there should be no restraint here. If a boy chose to bring in *Dindonneau aux truffes* or *Pieds de mouton à la Ste Menehould* to help out his tea, that was his lookout. Why should not the school grant a concession to some big London firm, who would pay handsomely for the privilege of supplying the hungry lads with every kind of expensive dainty? The sum could be justly made a large one, as any competing shop could be promptly put out of bounds with reason or without it. On one side, *confiserie*; at the other counter, charcuterie; enormous prices could be charged to the wealthy boys of whom the school would be composed. Yet, on the other hand, the distinguished visitor﻿—judge, bishop, peer or whatnot﻿—would lunch at the Headmaster’s house and eat the boys’ dinner and go away saying it was quite the plainest and very many times the best meal he had ever tasted. There would be well-hung saddle of mutton, roasted and not baked; floury potatoes and cauliflower; apple pudding with real English cheese, with an excellent glass of the school beer, an honest and delicious beverage made of malt and hops in the well-found school brewery. Horbury knew enough of modern eating and drinking to understand that such a meal would be a choice rarity to nine rich people out of ten; and yet it was “Spartan,” utterly devoid of luxury and ostentation.

===Chinese===
他又推及细部。学校要不要自办农场，好按成本价给学生供应肉、奶、面包、黄油和蔬菜——这问题他得请教行家。他相信办得到；他要从苏格兰低地物色一位农场主，聘为总管，薪水丰厚，还分红利。这广告何等堂皇：「全校膳食均由校办农场供应，由大卫·安德森（David Anderson）先生监理——安德森先生曾任哈丹纽克（Haddanneuk）农庄经理，该庄为艾尔公爵（Duke of Ayr）产业中最大的租佃。」伙食会更好、更便宜；但决不奢侈。「斯巴达式」这张牌永远值得打；在一个奢靡的时代，必须敲响简朴生活的调子；公学的旧日严厉一丝一毫不可丢失。另一方面，孩子们的手尽可以自由地伸进自己的钱袋；这一层不该有限制。哪个学生若愿带一客*Dindonneau aux truffes*（松露烩嫩鸡）或*Pieds de mouton à la Ste Menehould*（圣梅讷乌式煨羊蹄）来配他的茶点，那是他自己的事。学校为什么不可以向某家伦敦大商号出让特许权，让它出大价钱换取给饿瘪的小少爷们供应一切名贵美味的特权呢？这笔钱尽可以要得体面地高，因为任何一家竞争的铺子，都可以或凭道理、或不凭道理，立刻宣布为禁地。这边柜台摆*confiserie*（糖果蜜饯），那边柜台摆*charcuterie*（腌腊熟食）；反正学生个个家资豪富，价钱尽管往天上开。可另一方面，贵宾——法官、主教、勋爵之流——到校长宅里吃一顿午饭，吃的是学生伙食，临走赞不绝口：这是他平生吃过最朴素、又不知好过多少倍的一餐。那会是悬挂得法、烤而不焙的带骨羊里脊；起沙的土豆和花椰菜；苹果布丁配真正英国奶酪，外加一杯出色的校酿啤酒——麦芽蛇麻草在设备精良的学校酿造坊里酿出的诚实而甘美的饮料。霍伯里对现代的吃喝之道懂得够多，知道这样一顿饭，十个有钱人里有九个要当成稀世珍馐；可它又是「斯巴达式」的，绝无奢华与铺张。

===Original===
Again, he passed from detail and minutiae into great Napoleonic regions. A thousand boys at £500 a year; that would be an income for the school of five hundred thousand pounds! The profits would be gigantic, immense. After paying large, even extravagant, prices to the staff, after all building expenses had been deducted, he hardly dared to think how vast a sum would accrue year by year to the Trustees. The vision began to assume such magnificence that it became oppressive; it put on the splendours and delights of the hashish dream, which are too great and too piercing for mortal hearts to bear. And yet it was no mirage; there was not a step that could not be demonstrated, shown to be based on hard, matter-of-fact business considerations. He tried to keep back his growing excitement, to argue with himself that he was dealing in visions, but the facts were too obstinate. He saw that it would be his part to work the same miracle in the scholastic world as the great American storekeepers had operated in the world of retail trade. The principle was precisely the same: instead of a hundred small shops making comparatively modest and humdrum profits you had the vast emporium doing business on the gigantic scale with vastly diminished expenses and vastly increased rewards.

Here again was a hint. He had thought of America, and he knew that here was an inexhaustible gold mine, that no other scholastic prospector had even dreamed of. The rich American was notoriously hungry for everything that was English, from frock-coats to pedigrees. He had never thought of sending his son to an English Public School because he considered the system hopelessly behind the times. But the new translated Lupton would be to other Public Schools as a New York hotel of the latest fashion is to a village beer-shop. And yet the young millionaire would grow up in the company of the sons of the English gentlemen, imbibing the unique culture of English life, while at the same time he enjoyed all the advantages of modern ideas, modern science and modern business training. Land was still comparatively cheap at Lupton; the school must buy it quietly, indirectly, by degrees, and then pile after pile of vast buildings rose before his eyes. He saw the sons of the rich drawn from all the ends of the world to the Great School, there to learn the secret of the Anglo-Saxons.

===Chinese===
他又从细枝末节一跃而入拿破仑式的辽阔疆域。一千个学生，每人一年£500；这就是学校一年五十万镑的进项！利润将何其庞大，何其壮阔。给教师们开出巨额的、乃至挥霍性的薪酬之后，扣除一切建筑开支，每年将有多少巨款源源流入托管人的库房——他简直不敢想。幻象开始显出那等堂皇，反倒叫人喘不过气来；它披上了大麻幻梦的光华与极乐，那光华与极乐太浩大、太灼人，凡人的心受不住。然而它绝非海市蜃楼；没有一步不是可以论证的，没有一步不立于坚实、实际的经营盘算之上。他竭力按捺不断高涨的兴奋，跟自己争辩说他不过在纸上谈兵，可事实太固执了。他看清了：教育界里那个奇迹，正如美国大百货商人们在零售世界里所制造的奇迹，也该由他来施展。原则一模一样：一百家小店各自赚取平庸而寒酸的小利，如今换成一座巨无霸商场，以天文数字的规模营业，开支锐减，报酬暴增。

这里又是一条线索。他想到过美国，知道那儿有一座永不枯竭的金矿，别的教育界探矿者连梦都没有梦到过。美国阔佬以酷爱一切英国货闻名，从燕尾长礼服到家谱系牒，无所不爱。他一直没想到把儿子送进英国公学，是因为他觉得那套体制无可救药地落伍。可是经过点化的新卢普顿，之于其他公学，将如纽约最新式的大饭店之于乡村啤酒铺。而那位年轻的百万富翁，照样要在英国绅士们的公子中间长大，汲取英国人生而独具的文化，同时享有一切现代观念、现代科学与现代商业训练的好处。卢普顿的地价还比较便宜；学校必须悄悄地、间接地、一步一步把它收买下来；然后，一幢接一幢的巨大楼宇在他眼前拔地而起。他看见富豪们的儿子从天涯海角被吸引到这大学校里来，在这里修习盎格鲁-撒克逊人的奥秘。

===Original===
Chesson was mistaken in that idea of his, which he thought daring and original, of establishing a distinct Jewish House where the food should be “Kosher.” The rich Jew who desired to send his son to an English Public School was, in nine cases out of ten, anxious to do so precisely because he wanted to sink his son’s connection with Jewry in oblivion. He had heard Chesson talk of “our Christian duty to the seed of Israel” in this connection. The man was clearly a fool. No, the more Jews the better, but no Jewish House. And no Puseyism either: broad, earnest religious teaching, with a leaning to moderate Anglicanism, should be the faith of Lupton. As to this Chesson was, certainly, sound enough. He had always made a firm stand against ecclesiasticism in any form. Horbury knew the average English parent of the wealthier classes thoroughly; he knew that, though he generally called himself a Churchman, he was quite content to have his sons prepared for confirmation by a confessed Agnostic. Certainly this liberty must not be narrowed when Lupton became cosmopolitan. “We will retain all the dignified associations which belong to the Established Church,” he said to himself, “and at the same time we shall be utterly free from the taint of overemphasising dogmatic teaching.” He had a sudden brilliant idea. Everybody in Church circles was saying that the English bishops were terribly overworked, that it was impossible for the most strenuous men with the best intentions to supervise effectually the huge dioceses that had descended from the sparsely populated England of the Middle Ages. Everywhere there was a demand for suffragans and more suffragans. In the last week’s *Guardian* there were three letters on the subject, one from a clergyman in their own diocese. The Bishop had been attacked by some rabid ritualistic person, who had pointed out that nine out of every ten parishes had not so much as seen the colour of his hood ever since his appointment ten years before. The Archdeacon of Melby had replied in a capital letter, scathing and yet humorous. Horbury turned to the paper on the table beside his chair and looked up the letter. “In the first place,” wrote the Archdeacon, “your correspondent does not seem to have realised that the ethos of the Diocese of Melby is not identical with that of sacerdotalism. The sturdy folk of the Midlands have not yet, I am thankful to say, forgotten the lessons of our great Reformation. They have no wish to see a revival of the purely mechanical religion of the Middle Ages﻿—of the system of a sacrificing priesthood and of sacraments efficacious *ex opere operato*. Hence they do not regard the episcopate quite in the same light as your correspondent ‘Senex,’ who, it seems to me, looks upon a bishop as a sort of Christianised ‘medicine-man,’ endowed with certain mysterious thaumaturgic powers which have descended to him by an (imaginary) spiritual succession. This was not the view of Hooker, nor, I venture to say, has it ever been the view of the really representative divines of the Established Church of England.

===Chinese===
切森有一个自命大胆而独创的设想：专门辟一座犹太舍（Jewish House），膳食用「洁食」（Kosher）。在这一点上，切森想错了。有钱的犹太人想把儿子送进英国公学，十有八九正是巴不得叫儿子与犹太族裔的牵连从此湮没无闻。他曾听切森就此事说什么「我们对以色列苗裔的基督徒本分」。此人分明是个蠢货。不，犹太学生越多越好，只是不要犹太舍。也不要皮由兹主义（Puseyism）：宽广、恳切的宗教教育，略偏温和的安立甘主义，这才是卢普顿的信条。这一点上切森无疑还算稳健；他向来坚决抵制一切形式的教权主义。霍伯里把英国富家阶层的寻常家长摸得一清二楚；他知道，这班家长虽然口头上自称国教会信徒，却完全乐意叫一个自认的不可知论者来给儿子预备坚振礼。卢普顿一旦世界化，这种自由当然不可收窄。「国教会的全部体面渊源，我们一概保留，」他对自己说，「同时我们又要彻底免除过分强调教义灌输的恶臭。」他忽然灵光一闪。教会圈子里人人都说英国主教们劳累不堪；那些从中世纪人烟稀少的英国传下来的巨大教区，即便最勤勉、动机最纯正的人，也无法有效巡视。各处都在呼喊辅助主教，再要辅助主教。上星期的《卫报》上就有三封这方面的来信，其中一封出自本教区的一位教士。主教受到某个狂热的仪式主义者的攻击，那人指出，自从十年前就职以来，十个教区里倒有九个连他冠袍风帽的颜色都没见过。梅尔比（Melby）的副主教（Archdeacon）用一封极漂亮的信作了答复，犀利而诙谐。霍伯里转身拿过椅子旁小桌上的报纸，把那封信翻出来。「首先，」副主教写道，「贵报通信人似乎没有意识到，梅尔比教区的精神气质与司铎主义（sacerdotalism）的精神气质并不等同。谢天谢地，米德兰兹的坚毅民众还没有忘记我们伟大宗教改革的教训。他们不愿意看见中世纪那种纯然机械的宗教复活——那种献祭司铎的体制、那种*因功生效*（*ex opere operato*）的圣事。因此，他们对主教职位的看法，同贵报通信人『Senex』（拉丁语『老翁』，笔名）颇不一样；照我看来，后者把主教看作一种基督教化的『巫医』，天生被赋予了某些神秘的行神迹的法力，凭（想象中的）灵性传承世袭下来。这不是胡克（Hooker）的看法；我斗胆说，这也从来不是英国国教会真正有代表性的神学家们的看法。

===Original===
“Still,” the Archdeacon went on, “it must be admitted that the present diocese of Melby is unwieldy and, it may be fairly said, unworkable.”

Then there followed the humorous anecdote of Sir Boyle Roche and the Bird, and finally the Archdeacon emitted the prayer that God in His own good time would put it into the hearts of our rulers in Church and State to give their good Bishop an episcopal curate.

Horbury got up from his chair and paced up and down the study; his excitement was so great that he could keep quiet no longer. His cigar had gone out long ago, and he had barely sipped the whisky and soda. His eyes glittered with excitement.
